<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> For this STIM-family store-operated calcium sensor, which cellular component is its canonical intracellular membrane residence?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Endoplasmic reticulum<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Mitochondrion<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Golgi apparatus<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Lysosome<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: This protein is identified as a STIM-family (Stromal Interaction Molecule) store-operated calcium sensor. STIM proteins are canonical transmembrane proteins of the endoplasmic reticulum (ER). The sequence shows key features consistent with STIM proteins: an N-terminal transmembrane domain, a luminal domain containing EF-hand motifs (for Ca²⁺ sensing), a SAM domain, coiled-coil regions, and a C-terminal polybasic region (with clusters of Lys/Arg residues) that interacts with ORAI channels at ER-plasma membrane junctions. STIM proteins reside in the ER membrane where they sense the calcium content of the ER lumen. When ER Ca²⁺ stores are depleted, STIM proteins oligomerize and translocate to ER-plasma membrane junctions to activate ORAI channels (store-operated calcium entry). The canonical intracellular membrane residence of STIM proteins is the endoplasmic reticulum.<br><br>2. answer: A</td>
<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> The sequence is consistent with membrane association, but that feature alone does not identify a particular organelle. Which organelle is the canonical intracellular membrane residence of the protein represented by this sequence?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Endoplasmic reticulum<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Mitochondrion<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Golgi apparatus<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Lysosome<br><br><strong>Skills:</strong> GO_cellular_component_0044:1<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>## Reasoning<br><br>**Step 1: Identify the protein&#x27;s topology**<br><br>The sequence shows clear topological features:<br>- **N-terminal signal peptide**: MGRVSWIIALYLTINVVIVV followed by a cleavage site (NGD) → enters the secretory pathway<br>- **Large lumenal domain** (~200 amino acids) with various charged regions<br>- **Single transmembrane domain**: approximately &quot;DILLAFLALLLTSLIFLY&quot; (~14-17 hydrophobic residues)<br>- **Very long cytoplasmic tail** (~300+ residues) with abundant Ser/Thr (potential phosphorylation sites) and Pro-rich regions (SPPIPP)<br><br>**Step 2: Evaluate each option**<br><br>- **B. Mitochondrion**: Mitochondrial targeting uses amphipathic N-terminal sequences rich in basic residues — not classical hydrophobic signal peptides. This is ruled out.<br>- **D. Lysosome**: Lysosomal membrane proteins (LAMP1, LAMP2, CD68) have very short cytoplasmic tails (~10-20 residues). The ~300+ residue tail here is inconsistent.<br>- **A. ER**: ER single-pass membrane proteins typically have longer TM domains (19-25 residues) and often carry canonical retention signals (KKXX at the C-terminus). The TM domain here is shorter (~14-17 residues), and there is **no KKXX motif** at the C-terminus (ends with &quot;GTSN&quot;).<br>- **C. Golgi apparatus**: The combination of (1) shorter TM domain (~14-17 residues, favoring Golgi by length-based retention), (2) absence of ER retention signals, (3) very long cytoplasmic tail with multiple phosphorylation sites and Pro-rich regions (suggestive of trafficking/phosphorylation-dependent regulation), and (4) large lumenal domain — is most consistent with a **Golgi/TGN resident membrane protein**.<br><br>**Step 3: Conclusion**<br><br>The architecture (shorter TM domain, no ER retention motifs, extremely long cytoplasmic tail with signaling/regulatory features) is the hallmark of a canonical Golgi membrane protein.<br><br>answer: C</td>

<pre>The question asks about the STIM family (Stromal Interaction Molecule) of store-operated calcium sensors and their canonical intracellular membrane residence.

Let me analyze this:

1. STIM proteins (STIM1 and STIM2) are well-known calcium sensors in the cell. They are transmembrane proteins that sense calcium levels in the endoplasmic reticulum (ER) lumen.

2. The protein sequence provided contains:
   - An N-terminal signal peptide/transmembrane domain (MGRVSWIIALYLTINVVIVVNGDR...) - this is consistent with a type I membrane protein
   - EF-hand domain (EF-hand domains typically look like DKNDNDKKD or similar sequences with the pattern -x-xx-D-x-D)
   - SAM domain (Sterile Alpha Motif)
   - Coiled-coil regions
   - C-terminal polybasic region with lysine/arginine residues (KKRK...)

3. STIM proteins are canonical ER membrane proteins. Their structure includes:
   - A single transmembrane helix near the N-terminus
   - A luminal domain containing EF-hand and SAM domains that sense ER Ca²⁺ levels
   - Cytosolic domains that interact with ORAI channels at ER-plasma membrane junctions

4. The question itself identifies this as a &quot;STIM-family store-operated calcium sensor,&quot; which confirms this is a STIM protein.

5. Among the choices:
   - A. Endoplasmic reticulum - This is the canonical location of STIM proteins
   - B. Mitochondrion - Not the canonical location
   - C. Golgi apparatus - Not the canonical location
   - D. Lysosome - Not the canonical location

The answer is clearly A. Endoplasmic reticulum.
